{"step_index":75,"source":"MODEL","type":"PLANNER_RESPONSE","status":"DONE","created_at":"2026-05-05T09:24:59Z","content":"That is a great question. On the surface, it feels like they might be measuring the same thing (inconsistency), but they actually measure two completely different types of risk. \n\nHere is the difference and why keeping SDI is incredibly valuable for your prediction engine:\n\n### 1. Scoring Volatility = *Past Output Consistency*\n**Scoring Volatility** (which you have displayed on the dashboard) looks at the macroscopic team level. It measures the standard deviation of a team's total points from game to game.\n*   **Low Volatility** means a team consistently scores near their average (e.g., always between 80 and 88 points).\n*   **High Volatility** means their scoring wildly fluctuates (e.g., 70 one night, 105 the next).\n\n### 2. SDI (Star Dependency Index) = *Structural Fragility*\n**SDI** looks under the hood at the microscopic player level. It measures how heavily that total team score relies on just 1 or 2 specific players. \n<truncated 1213 bytes>"}

{"step_index":85,"source":"MODEL","type":"PLANNER_RESPONSE","status":"DONE","created_at":"2026-05-05T09:45:07Z","content":"You are completely right that the negative signs can be confusing. **Team Bias (±Δ)** is one of the most powerful metrics you have, but it requires a quick mental flip to read it correctly.\n\nHere is exactly how to interpret it.\n\n### The Math Behind Bias\nTeam Bias measures the **directional error** of your model for a specific team. It is calculated as:\n`Actual Score - Model's Predicted Score = Bias`\n\n### How to Read the Signs\n\n**1. Negative Bias (e.g., -6.1)**\n*   **What it means:** The actual score is *lower* than the model predicted.\n*   **Plain English:** **The model is consistently OVERESTIMATING this team.** They are chronically underperforming the model's expectations by 6.1 points per game.\n*   *Actionable takeaway:* If you see a heavily negative bias, you should mentally (or systematically) subtract points from their projected score. They are a great candidate for team unders.\n\n**2. Positive Bias (e.g., +4.5)**\n*   **What it means:** The actual score is *higher* than the model predicted.\n<truncated 1831 bytes>"}

{"step_index":88,"source":"MODEL","type":"PLANNER_RESPONSE","status":"DONE","created_at":"2026-05-05T10:06:05Z","content":"You should use a **straight calculation (adding them together)**, which means in this case, you subtract the full 10 points. \n\nHere is why you combine them rather than average them:\n\nA game's total score is simply `Team A's Score + Team B's Score`. \n\nBecause your model generates a specific projected score for *each individual team*, the bias applies individually to each team's contribution to the total:\n\n*   **Hapoel Tel Aviv** is projected for 116.3 points. Their bias is -6.1. This means they are likely to actually score around **110.2** (116.3 - 6.1).\n*   **Real Madrid** is projected for 116.2 points. Their bias is -3.9. This means they are likely to actually score around **112.3** (116.2 - 3.9).\n\nIf you add those bias-adjusted scores together (110.2 + 112.3), you get a new expected total of **222.5**. \n\nIf you just took the average of the biases (subtracting ~5), you would end up with 227.5, which essentially ignores half of the predictive error the model is making!\n\n### The Golden Rule for Game Totals:\n<truncated 471 bytes>"}

Because **Team MAE** and **Scoring Volatility** are specific to that individual team, you must compare them to that individual team's **Pts/Game**. \n\nYou do not compare them to the Model Total, because the Model Total is the combined score of *both* teams.\n\n### Here is why:\nImagine an extreme example where an NBA team (averaging 120 points) plays a college team (averaging 60 points). The Model Total for the game would be roughly **180**.\n\n*   If the college team has an MAE of **12**, that is terrible for them. {"step_index":1071,"source":"MODEL","type":"PLANNER_RESPONSE","status":"DONE","created_at":"2026-05-05T20:56:00Z","content":"Yes, it handles the negative signs mathematically, and it's actually one of the smartest parts of the scoring engine!\n\nIn the code, the bias is calculated like this:\n```python\navg_bias_abs = abs((game[\"bias_a\"] + game[\"bias_b\"]) / 2)\n```\n\nBecause it adds the raw signed numbers *before* taking the absolute value, **biases can compound or cancel each other out** depending on their signs.\n\nHere is exactly how your example of `-6` and `-5` works:\n\n### Scenario 1: Compounding Bias (Both Negative)\n* **Home Bias:** `-6.0` (Model consistently undershoots them by 6)\n* **Away Bias:** `-5.0` (Model consistently undershoots them by 5)\n* **Calculation:** `(-6.0 + -5.0) / 2 = -5.5`\n* **Absolute Value:** `5.5`\n* **Result:** `5.5` falls into the `≤ 6.0` tier, awarding **10 out of 20 points**.\n* *Why this is good:* The model is systematically undershooting *both* teams, meaning the projected game total is highly likely to be too low. The system recognizes this systemic leak and penalizes the score.\n\n<truncated 618 bytes>"}

{"step_index":1074,"source":"MODEL","type":"PLANNER_RESPONSE","status":"DONE","created_at":"2026-05-05T20:59:17Z","content":"Yes, exactly! The `-` is converted to a `+` **at the very end of the calculation** (using the mathematical absolute value `abs()`). \n\nThis is because we don't care *which direction* the model is wrong (whether it's predicting way too high or way too low). We only care about the *severity* of the error. A game that is systematically off by 17 points is terrible to bet on, regardless of whether it's +17 or -17.\n\nHere is how `-20` and `-15` would be calculated:\n\n### Extreme Negative Bias Example\n* **Home Bias:** `-20.0`\n* **Away Bias:** `-15.0`\n* **Calculation:** `(-20.0 + -15.0) / 2 = -17.5`\n* **Absolute Value:** `17.5` *(The minus sign is stripped away here)*\n* **Result:** `17.5` is greater than 8.0, so it falls into the worst tier and awards **0 out of 20 points**.\n\n<truncated 281 bytes>"}

{"step_index":1235,"source":"MODEL","type":"PLANNER_RESPONSE","status":"DONE","created_at":"2026-05-06T07:36:07Z","content":"Yes, absolutely. **Volatility is the bedrock of the entire totals prediction engine.**\n\nThink of it like building a house. Volatility is the foundation. If the foundation is built on sand (high volatility), nothing else matters. If the foundation is solid rock (low volatility), you can fix everything else.\n\nHere is how the four metrics relate to each other:\n\n1. **Volatility answers:** *\"Are these teams even predictable in the first place?\"*\nIf a team scores 120 points on Tuesday and 80 points on Thursday, they might *average* 100 points, but they will almost never actually score 100 points. If Volatility is bad, then betting their totals is just flipping a coin, no matter how good the other metrics look. \n\n2. **MAE answers:** *\"Has the model historically predicted them accurately?\"*\nIf Volatility is good, a good MAE confirms the model has them perfectly dialed in.\n\n3. **Bias answers:** *\"When the model misses, does it miss in a predictable direction?\"*\n<truncated 754 bytes>"}

Here is the problem in numbers:\n\n### Current Spread Weight — Too Powerful\nThe total max raw score is **75 points** (Vol:20 + MAE:15 + Bias:25 + Spread:15). Spread accounts for **20% of the total score**, and with the new precision bands the swing between worst (5pts) and best (15pts) is **10 raw points** — easily enough to jump a full confidence tier on its own. That makes it a co-primary metric, not a secondary one.\n\n### My Suggestion — Compress the Spread Range\n\nKeep the same band logic, but **halve the max and shrink the swing** so spread can only nudge by ±5 points at most:\n\n```python\n< 2  pts  → 2   # Worst zone — foul-fest / OT trap\n2-5  pts  → 5   # Below average\n5-11 pts  → 8   # Sweet spot\n11-15 pts → 2   # Surprise drop\n> 15 pts  → 5   # Blowout recovery\n```\n\n**Why this works:**\n- Max spread contribution: **8/75 = 10.7%** (down from 20%)\n- Maximum swing: **6 points** (down from 10)\n<truncated 556 bytes>"}
